| [Kurdish Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_Hezbollah) (sometimes referred as Turkish Hezbollah) or just *Hizbullah* in Turkey, is a mainly [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) [Islamist](15012.md) militant organization, active against the [Kurdistan Workers' Party](69680.md) (PKK) and the [Government of Turkey](3680197.md).[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55] |  |

From 1978 until 2025, the [Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Turkey) was in an armed conflict with the [Kurdistan Workers' Party](69680.md) (PKK) ([Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_languages): *Partiya Karkerên Kurdistanê*) as well as its allied insurgent groups, both Kurdish and non-Kurdish.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] The initial core demand of the PKK was its [separation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_separatism) from Turkey to create an independent [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan).[^cite_note-Freedom_Falcons-42] In 2010, the PKK abandoned separatism in favor of [autonomy](191145.md)[^cite_note-60] and/or greater [political and cultural rights for Kurds inside the Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_of_Kurdish_people_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-61]

Although most of the conflict took place in [Northern Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Kurdistan), which corresponded with southeastern Turkey,[^cite_note-IDMC-62] the Kurdish-Turkish conflict spread to many other regions.[^cite_note-63] The PKK's presence in [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) resulted in the [Turkish Armed Forces](30205.md) carrying out frequent ground incursions and air and artillery strikes in the region,[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] and its influence in [Syrian Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Kurdistan) led to similar activity there. The conflict cost the [economy of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Turkey) an estimated [\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_dollar)300 to 450 billion, mostly in military costs. It also had negative effects on [tourism in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-Turkey's_safety-68][^cite_note-crisis-69]

A revolutionary group, the PKK was founded in 1978 in the village of Fis, [Lice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lice,_Turkey) by a group of Kurdish students led by [Abdullah Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_%C3%96calan).[^cite_note-70] The initial reason given by the PKK for this was the [oppression of Kurds in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_of_Kurdish_people_in_Turkey).[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] At the time, the use of [Kurdish language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_languages), dress, [folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore), and names were banned in Kurdish-inhabited areas.[^cite_note-hannum-73] In an attempt to [deny their existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denial_of_Kurds_by_Turkey), the Turkish government categorized [Kurds](17068.md) as "Mountain Turks" during the 1930s and 1940s.[^cite_note-hannum-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] The words "Kurds", "[Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan)", or "Kurdish" were officially banned by the Turkish government.[^cite_note-76] Following the [military coup of 1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), the Kurdish language was officially prohibited in public and private life until 1991.[^cite_note-NYTK-77] Many who spoke, published, or sang in Kurdish were arrested and imprisoned.[^cite_note-78]

The PKK was formed in an effort to establish linguistic, cultural, and political rights for Turkey's Kurdish minority.[^cite_note-79] However, the full-scale [insurgency](589841.md) did not begin until 15 August 1984, when the PKK announced a Kurdish uprising. Between 1984 and 2012, an estimated 40,000 had died, the majority of whom were Turkish Kurdish civilians.[^cite_note-80] Both sides were accused of numerous [human rights](13831.md) [abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuses). The [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) has condemned Turkey for thousands of human rights abuses.[^cite_note-ETCHR1-81][^cite_note-AnnualETCHR-82] Many judgments are related to the systematic executions of Kurdish civilians,[^cite_note-hum1-83] torture,[^cite_note-\:1-84] forced displacements,[^cite_note-85] destroyed villages,[^cite_note-St._Martin's_Press-86][^cite_note-Univ_Of_Texas_Press-87][^cite_note-Taylor_and_Francis-88] [arbitrary arrests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_arrest),[^cite_note-autogenerated1-89] and the [forced disappearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_disappearance) or murder of Kurdish journalists, activists and politicians.[^cite_note-khrp.org-90][^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] Teachers who provided and students who demanded education in Kurdish language were prosecuted and sentenced for supporting terrorism of the PKK.[^cite_note-93] Similarly, the PKK had faced international condemnation, mainly by Turkish allies, for using [terrorist tactics](30636.md), which include civilian [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre), [summary executions](1227309.md), [suicide bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_bomber), and [child soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_soldier), and involvement in [drug trafficking](218449.md).[^cite_note-\:11-94][^cite_note-hrwf.eu-95]

In February 1999, PKK leader Abdullah Öcalan was arrested in [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya by a group of special forces personnel[^cite_note-96] and taken to Turkey, where [he remains in prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment_of_Abdullah_%C3%96calan) on an island in the [Sea of Marmara](28863.md).[^cite_note-97] The first insurgency lasted until March 1993, when the PKK declared a [unilateral ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Kurdistan_Workers'_Party_ceasefire).[^cite_note-ceasefires-98] Fighting resumed the same year.[^cite_note-\:17-99] In 2013, the Turkish government started talks with Öcalan. Following mainly secret negotiations, a largely successful ceasefire was put in place by both the [Turkish state and the PKK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013%E2%80%932015_PKK%E2%80%93Turkey_peace_process). On 21 March 2013, Öcalan announced the "end of armed struggle" and a ceasefire with peace talks.[^cite_note-End_of_armed_struggle_5-4]

The rise of [Islamic State](9087364.md) on Turkey's southern border illuminated diverging interests and ignited new tensions. In response to Islamic State's 2015 [Suruç bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suru%C3%A7_bombing) on Turkish soil, the [Ceylanpınar incidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceylanp%C4%B1nar_incidents) saw the killing of two Turkish police officers by suspected PKK militants and the return to open conflict.[^cite_note-\:32-100][^cite_note-Ceasefire_and_peace-101] Subsequently, the conflict [resulted in](#Casualties) about 8,000 killed in Turkey alone, with about 20,000 more in Syria and Iraq due to Turkish military operations. Numerous human rights violations occurred, including torture and widespread destruction of property.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-turkeyhrw-103] Substantial parts of many Kurdish-majority cities including [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r), [Şırnak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C4%B1rnak), [Mardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardin), [Cizre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cizre), [Nusaybin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusaybin), and [Yüksekova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C3%BCksekova) were destroyed in the clashes[^cite_note-104] or external operations.

[New peace process](79612363.md) discussions began in 2024. In early 2025, Öcalan called PKK to disarm.[^cite_note-105] On 12 May 2025, the PKK announced its full dissolution to favor political means.[^cite_note-106] However, Turkey's military will continue operations against the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) in regions where it remains active, despite the group's announcement of its dissolution.[^cite_note-theinsightinternational.com-107][^cite_note-The_Hindu-108][^cite_note-thenewregion.com-109][^cite_note-rudaw\.net-110]

## History

### Beginnings

In 1977, a small group under Öcalan's leadership released a declaration on Kurdish identity in Turkey. The group, which called itself the *Revolutionaries of Kurdistan* also included [Ali Haydar Kaytan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Haydar_Kaytan), [Cemil Bayik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cemil_Bayik), [Haki Karer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haki_Karer) and [Kemal Pir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_Pir).[^cite_note-PK_1995-111] The group decided in 1974[^cite_note-security-112] to start a campaign for Kurdish rights. Cemil Bayik was sent to [Urfa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urfa), Kemal Pir to [Mus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu%C5%9F), Haki Karer to [Batman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batman,_Turkey), and Ali Haydar Kaytan to [Tunceli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunceli). They then started student organisations that contacted local workers and farmers to raise awareness about Kurdish rights.[^cite_note-PK_1995-111]

In 1977, an assembly was held to evaluate these political activities. The assembly included 100 people from different backgrounds and several representatives of other leftist organisations. In spring 1977, Öcalan travelled to [Mount Ararat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Ararat), [Erzurum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erzurum), Tunceli, [Elazig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elazig), [Antep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antep), and other cities to make the public aware of the group's activities. This was followed by a Turkish government crackdown against the organisation. On 18 May 1977, Haki Karer was assassinated in Antep. During this period, the group was also targeted by the Turkish ultranationalist organization, the [Nationalist Movement Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_Movement_Party)'s [Grey Wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_Wolves_\(organization\)). Some wealthy Kurdish landowners targeted the group as well, killing Halil Çavgun on 18 May 1978, which resulted in large protest meetings in Erzurum, [Dersim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dersim), Elazig, and Antep.[^cite_note-PK_1995-111]

The founding Congress of the PKK was held on 27 November 1978 in Fis, a village near the city of [Lice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lice,_Turkey). During this congress, the 25 people present decided to found the Kurdistan Workers' Party. The Turkish state, Turkish rightist groups, and some Kurdish landowners continued their attacks on the group. In response, the PKK organised and armed members to protect itself, thus becoming more involved in the [fighting between leftist and rightist groups in Turkey (1978–1980)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence_in_Turkey,_1970s).[^cite_note-PK_1995-111] During this time, the right-wing [Grey Wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_Wolves_\(organization\)) militia killed 109 and injured 176 [Alevi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alevi) Kurds in the town of [Kahramanmaraş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahramanmara%C5%9F) on 25 December 1978 in what would become known as the [Maraş Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara%C5%9F_Massacre).[^cite_note-david-113] In Summer 1979, Öcalan travelled to [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md) where he made contacts with Syrian and [Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people) leaders.[^cite_note-PK_1995-111] After the [Turkish coup d'état on 12 September 1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and a crackdown which was launched on all political organisations,[^cite_note-Gil-114] during which at least 191 people were killed[^cite_note-115] and half a million were imprisoned,[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-118] most of the PKK withdrew into Syria and Lebanon. Öcalan went to Syria in September 1980 with Kemal Pir, [Mahsum Korkmaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsum_Korkmaz), and Delil Dogan being sent to set up an organisation in Lebanon. Some PKK fighters took part in the [1982 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Lebanon_War) on the Syrian side.[^cite_note-119]

The Second PKK Party Congress was then held in [Daraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daraa), Syria, from 20 to 25 August 1982. Here it was decided that the organisation would return to [Turkish Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Kurdistan) to start an armed guerilla war there for the creation of an independent Kurdish state. Meanwhile, they prepared guerrilla forces in Syria and Lebanon to go to war. However, many PKK leaders were arrested in Turkey and sent to [Diyarbakır Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r_Prison). Because of the treatment of the prisoners, the prison became the site of much political protest.[^cite_note-PK_1995-111] (See also [Torture in Turkey#Deaths in custody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_in_Turkey#Deaths_in_custody).)

In Diyarbakır Prison, PKK member [Mazlum Doğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazlum_Do%C4%9Fan) burned himself to death on 21 March 1982, in protest at the treatment in prison. Ferhat Kurtay, Necmi Önen, Mahmut Zengin and Eşref Anyık followed his example on 17 May. On 14 July, PKK members Kemal Pir, M. Hayri Durmuş, Ali Çiçek and Akif Yılmaz started a [hunger strike](222292.md) in Diyarbakır Prison.[^cite_note-120] In 1983, the conflict reached [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) as the [Kurdistan Region–PKK conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Region%E2%80%93PKK_conflict). Kemal Pir died on 7 September, M. Hayri Durmuş on the 12th, Akif Yılmaz on the 15th, and Ali Çiçek on the 17th. On 13 April 1984, a 75-day hunger strike started in Istanbul. As a result, four prisoners—Abdullah Meral, Haydar Başbağ, Fatih Ökütülmüş, and Hasan Telci—died.[^cite_note-121]

On 25 October 1986, the third Congress was held in [Beqaa Valley](2162385.md), Lebanon. Issues raised included lack of discipline, growing internal criticism, and splinter groups within the organization. This had led the organisation to execute some internal critics, especially ex-members who had joined [Tekosin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tekosin?action=edit\&redlink=1), a rival Marxist–Leninist organization. Öcalan strongly criticized the leaders responsible for the guerrilla forces during the early 1980s and threatened others with the death penalty, if they joined rival groups or refused to obey orders. The PKK's military defeats meant they were no closer to their primary goal of an independent [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan). Cooperation with criminals and [dictators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictator) had tarnished the organization's image. During the Congress, the leaders decided to advance the armed struggle, increase the number of fighters, and dissolve the HRK, which was replaced by the [Kurdistan Popular Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Popular_Liberation_Army?action=edit\&redlink=1) (ARGK). A newly established [Mahsum Korkmaz Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsum_Korkmaz_Academy), a politico-military academy, replaced the [Helve Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helve_Camp?action=edit\&redlink=1), and a new military [conscription](5735.md) policy was approved, which obliged every family to send someone to the guerrilla forces.[^cite_note-davidp1-122][^cite_note-natpol1-123][^cite_note-124]

The decisions that were taken during the third Congress transformed the PKK from a Leninist organization into one in which power was more concentrated, as Öcalan accrued more Önderlik (leadership). Some of the reasons why Öcalan took power from the other leaders, such as [Murat Karayilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Karayilan), [Cemil Bayik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cemil_Bayik) and [Duran Kalkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duran_Kalkan), were growing internal conflict and the organization's inability to stop it. According to Michael Gunter, Öcalan, even before this time, had already carried out a purge of many rival PKK members, who were tortured and forced to confess they were traitors before being executed. [Ibrahim Halik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Halik?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Mehmet Ali Cetiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmet_Ali_Cetiner?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Mehmet Result Altinok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmet_Result_Altinok?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Saime Askin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saime_Askin?action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Ayten Yildirim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayten_Yildirim?action=edit\&redlink=1) were some of the victims. Later in 2006, Öcalan denied these accusations and stated in his book that both [Mahsum Korkmaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsum_Korkmaz), the first supreme military commander of the PKK, and [Engin Sincer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engin_Sincer?action=edit\&redlink=1), a high-ranking commander, likely died as a result of internal conflicts and described the perpetrators as "gangs". Leaked reports, however, revealed the [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) personality of Öcalan, who had brutally suppressed dissent and purged opponents since the early 1980s. According to David L. Philips, up to sixty PKK members were executed in 1986, including Mahsum Korkmaz, who he believes was murdered on 28 March 1986. Between the 1980 and 1990, the organization targeted defectors, assassinating two of them in Sweden, two in the Netherlands, three in Germany and one in Denmark.[^cite_note-natpol1-123][^cite_note-125]

In 1990, during the fourth Congress, the PKK ended its unpopular conscription policy. The organization's attempts to take into the account the demands and criticism of its support base had helped it to increase its popularity. According to Stanton, the PKK's improved relationship with its civilian base likely created incentives for the government to engage in [state terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_terrorism) against some of its Kurdish citizens. The PKK was rarely able to prevent this.[^cite_note-civtar1-126]

### First insurgency

#### 1984–1993



[OHAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OHAL) region—defining areas in Turkey under a state of emergency—in red with neighbouring provinces in orange, 1987–2002

The PKK launched its insurgency against the Turkish state on 15 August 1984[^cite_note-PK_1995-111][^cite_note-under_pressure-127] with [armed attacks on Eruh and Semdinli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15_August_1984_PKK_attacks). During these attacks, one [Turkish Gendarmerie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Gendarmerie) soldier was killed and seven soldiers, two policemen and three civilians injured. It was followed by a PKK raid on a police station in [Siirt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siirt), two days later.[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128]

In the early 1990s, President [Turgut Özal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turgut_%C3%96zal) agreed to negotiations with the PKK, after the 1991 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) changed the geopolitical dynamics in the region. Apart from Özal, himself half-Kurdish, few Turkish politicians were interested in a peace process, nor was more than a part of the PKK itself.[^cite_note-129] In February 1991, during the presidency of Özal, the prohibition of Kurdish music was ended.[^cite_note-130]

In 1993, Özal returned to working on a peace plan with the former finance minister [Adnan Kahveci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adnan_Kahveci) and the General Commander of the Turkish Gendarmerie, [Eşref Bitlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E%C5%9Fref_Bitlis).[^cite_note-131]

### Unilateral cease-fire (1993)

Negotiations led to a unilateral [cease-fire by the PKK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Kurdistan_Workers'_Party_ceasefire) on 17 March 1993. Accompanied by [Jalal Talabani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalal_Talabani) at a press conference in [Barelias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barelias), Lebanon, Öcalan stated that the PKK no longer sought a separate state, but peace,[^cite_note-\:0-132] dialogue, and free political action for Kurds in Turkey within the framework of a democratic state. [Süleyman Demirel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleyman_Demirel), the prime minister of Turkey at the time, refused to negotiate with the PKK, but also stated that forced [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) was the wrong approach towards the Kurds.[^cite_note-133] Several Kurdish politicians supported the ceasefire, and [Kemal Burkay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_Burkay) and [Ahmet Türk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmet_T%C3%BCrk) of the [People's Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Labor_Party) (HEP) were also present at the press conference.[^cite_note-\:0-132]

With the PKK's ceasefire declaration in hand, Özal was planning to propose a major pro-Kurdish reform package at the next meeting of the [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Council_\(Turkey\)). The president's death on 17 April led to the postponement of that meeting, and the plans were never presented.[^cite_note-134] A Turkish army attack on the PKK on 19 May 1993 in [Kulp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kulp,_Turkey)[^cite_note-\:02-135] brought the ceasefire to an end. Five days later, the PKK carried out the [Çewlik massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87ewlik_massacre). Former PKK commander turned whistleblower [Şemdin Sakık](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eemdin_Sak%C4%B1k) has said that the massacre had been allowed to go ahead by the Turkish military, and was part of the [Doğu Çalışma Grubu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Do%C4%9Fu_%C3%87al%C4%B1%C5%9Fma_Grubu)'s coup plans.[^cite_note-TZ-136] On 8 June 1993, Öcalan announced the end of the PKK ceasefire.[^cite_note-\:17-99]

### Insurgency (1993–1995)

Under the new Presidency of [Süleyman Demirel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleyman_Demirel) and Premiership of [Tansu Çiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansu_%C3%87iller), the [Castle Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Plan) (to use any and all violent means to solve the Kurdish question), which Özal had opposed, was enacted, and the peace process abandoned.[^cite_note-TIHV-137] Some journalists and politicians maintain that Özal's death (allegedly by poison), along with the assassination of a number of political and military figures supporting his peace efforts, was part of [a covert military coup in 1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_alleged_Turkish_military_coup) aimed at stopping the peace plans.

To counter the PKK, the Turkish military started new [counter-insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-insurgency) strategies between 1992 and 1995. To deprive the rebels of a logistical base of operations and punish local people supporting the PKK, the military carried out [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) of the countryside and [destroyed over 3,000 Kurdish villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_villages_depopulated_by_Turkey), causing at least 2 million refugees. Most of these villages were evacuated, but other villages were burned, bombed, or shelled by government forces, and several villages were obliterated from the air. While some were destroyed or evacuated, other villages agreed to join the side of the government. The state offered salaries to local farmers and shepherds to join the [Village Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Village_Guards), to prevent the PKK from operating in these villages. Villages which refused to cooperate were evacuated by the military. These tactics managed to drive the rebels from the cities and villages into the mountains, although they still often launched reprisals on pro-government villages, which included attacks on civilians.[^cite_note-138] Turkish armed forces reported that on 26 May 1994, the Turkish Air Force targeted the Kurdistan Workers' Party (PKK) militants by bombing North Iraq, and killing 79 militants.[^cite_note-139] During [Newroz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newroz_as_celebrated_by_Kurds) (the Kurdish New Year) on 20 March 1995, the Turkish military launched [another cross-border operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Steel) against the PKK in Iraq to prevent further border station ambushes conducted by the PKK. A force of 35,000 personnel (in which most were there for pulling security, a very small portion took part in the actual fighting) went into [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md), assisted by planes, helicopters, tanks and APCs. The operation ended in a Turkish victory as the [Zap Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Zab) was captured and destroyed by Turkish forces. 555 PKK members were killed and another were 13 captured.[^cite_note-140]

### Unilateral ceasefire (1995–1996)

In December 1995, the PKK announced a second unilateral ceasefire, ahead of the [general elections on 24 December 1995](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Turkish_general_election). This was aimed at giving the new Turkish government time to articulate a way of resolving the conflict. During the ceasefire, [civil society](209443.md) groups organized several peace initiatives in support of a solution to the conflict. But in May 1996, there was an attempt to assassinate Abdullah Öcalan in [Damascus](8914.md), and in June of the same year the Turkish military began to pursue the PKK into [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md).[^cite_note-\:6-141] The PKK announced the end of the unilateral ceasefire on 16 August 1996, stating that it was still ready for peace negotiations as a political solution.[^cite_note-\:6-141]

### Insurgency (1996–1999)

One of the turning points in the conflict was when Turkey did the largest cross-border mission in its history. [Operation Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Hammer_\(1997\)) was done in May 1997 and over fifty thousand Turkish soldiers and ten thousand village guards took part in the operation. The operation was successful as the Turkish military killed over 3,000 insurgents and captured more than 400 in just two months and destroyed almost all of the Kurdish camps in Northern Iraq with just 114 casualties. Another turning point in the conflict[^cite_note-hurriyet-142] came in 1998, when, after political pressure and military threats[^cite_note-KDP-143] from the Turkish government against Syria, the PKK's leader, Abdullah Öcalan, was forced to leave Syria, where he had been in exile since September 1980. He first went to [Russia](25391.md), then to [Italy](14532.md) and [Greece](12108.md). He was eventually brought to the Greek embassy in [Nairobi](21482.md), Kenya. After leaving the embassy on 15 February 1999 for the airport, he was kidnapped in a joint [MİT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Organization_\(Turkey\))-[CIA](5183633.md) operation and brought to Turkey,[^cite_note-nytimes-capture-144] which resulted in [major protests by Kurds worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_1999_Kurdish_protests).[^cite_note-KDP-143] Three Kurdish protestors were shot dead when trying to enter the Israeli consulate in Berlin to protest alleged Israeli involvement in his capture.[^cite_note-145] Although the capture of Öcalan ended a third cease-fire which Öcalan had declared on 1 August 1998, on 1 September 1999[^cite_note-ceasefires-98] the PKK declared a unilateral cease-fire which would last until 2004.[^cite_note-security-112]

### Unilateral cease-fire (1999–2003)



KADEK flag



KONGRA-GEL flag

After the unilateral cease-fire the PKK declared in September 1999, their forces fully withdrew from Turkish Kurdistan, set up new bases in the [kandil Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qandil_Mountains) of [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md),[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128] and in February 2000 they declared the formal end of the war.[^cite_note-KDP-143] After this, the PKK said it would switch its strategy to using peaceful methods to achieve their objectives. In April 2002, the PKK changed its name to *KADEK* ([Kurdistan Freedom and Democracy Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Freedom_and_Democracy_Congress?action=edit\&redlink=1)), claiming the PKK had fulfilled its mission and would now move on as purely political organisation.[^cite_note-ataa-146] In October 2003 the KADEK announced its dissolution and declared the creation of a new organisation: *KONGRA-GEL* ([Kurdistan Peoples Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Peoples_Congress?action=edit\&redlink=1)).[^cite_note-new_pkk-147]

Offers by the PKK for negotiations were ignored by the Turkish government,[^cite_note-ataa-146] which claimed that the KONGRA-GEL continued to carry out armed attacks in the 1999–2004 period, although not on the same scale as before September 1999. They also blame the KONGRA-GEL for Kurdish riots which happened during the period.[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128] The PKK argues that all of its military activity during this period was defensive, as the Turkish military launched some 700 raids against their bases, including in Northern Iraq.[^cite_note-under_pressure-127] Despite the KONGRA-GEL cease-fire, other groups continued their armed activities. For example, the [Revolutionary Party of Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Party_of_Kurdistan) (PŞK) tried to use the cease-fire to attract PKK fighters to join their organisation.[^cite_note-148] The [Kurdistan Freedom Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Freedom_Hawks) (TAK) were formed during this period by radical KONGRA-GEL commanders dissatisfied with the cease-fire.[^cite_note-149] The period after the capture of Öcalan was used by the Turkish government to launch major crackdown operations against the [Turkish Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Hezbollah) (Kurdish Hezbollah), arresting 3,300 Hizbullah members in 2000, compared to 130 in 1998, and killing the group's leader Hüseyin Velioğlu on 13 January 2000.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-turkishweekly-151][^cite_note-152] During this phase of the war at least 145 people were killed during fighting between the PKK and security forces.[^cite_note-153]

After the [AK Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK_Party) came to power in 2002, the Turkish state started to ease restrictions on the Kurdish language and culture.[^cite_note-154]

From 2003 to 2004 there was a power struggle inside the KONGRA-GEL between a reformist wing which wanted the organisation to disarm completely and a traditionalist wing which wanted the organisation to resume its armed insurgency once again.[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128][^cite_note-Cemil_Bayik-155] The conservative wing of the organisation won this power struggle[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128] forcing reformist leaders such as [Kani Yilmaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kani_Yilmaz), [Nizamettin Tas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizamettin_Tas) and Abdullah Öcalan's younger brother [Osman Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_%C3%96calan) to leave the organisation.[^cite_note-Cemil_Bayik-155] The three major traditionalist leaders, [Murat Karayilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Karayilan), [Cemil Bayik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cemil_Bayik) and [Bahoz Erdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahoz_Erdal) formed the new leadership committee of the organisation.[^cite_note-156] The new administration decided to restart the insurgency, because they claimed that without guerillas the PKK's political activities would remain unsuccessful.[^cite_note-ataa-146][^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128] This came as the pro-Kurdish [People's Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democracy_Party) (HADEP) was banned by the Turkish Supreme Court on 13 March 2003[^cite_note-157] and its leader [Murat Bozlak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Bozlak) was imprisoned.[^cite_note-158]

In April 2005, KONGRA-GEL changed its name back to PKK.[^cite_note-new_pkk-147] Because not all of the KONGRA-GEL's elements accepted this, the organisation has also been referred to as the New PKK.[^cite_note-159] The KONGRA-GEL has since become the Legislative Assembly of the [Kurdistan Communities Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Communities_Union), a pan-Kurdish umbrella organisation which includes the PKK. Ex-[DEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Party_\(Turkey\)) member [Zübeyir Aydar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%BCbeyir_Aydar) is the president of the KONGRA-GEL.[^cite_note-160]

Through the cease-fire years 2000–2003, some 711 people were killed, according to the Turkish government.[^cite_note-Turkish_casualties-161] The Uppsala Conflict Data Program put casualties during these years at 368 to 467 killed.[^cite_note-UCDP-162]

### Insurgency (2004–2012)

#### 2003–2005



PKK supporters in London, April 2003

In September 2003, the PKK declared an end to its cease-fire, but waited until mid-2004 before going on the offensive again.[^cite_note-163] In June 2004, the PKK resumed its armed activities because they claimed the Turkish government was ignoring their calls for negotiations and was still attacking their forces.[^cite_note-ataa-146][^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128] The government claimed that in that same month, some 2,000 Kurdish guerrillas entered Turkey via Iraqi Kurdistan.[^cite_note-security-112] The PKK, now lacking Syrian government support and the manpower they had in the 1990s, took up new tactics. It reduced the size of its field units from 15 to 20 fighters to teams of 6–8, and avoided direct confrontations, relying more on the use of [landmines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmine), [snipers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sniper) and small [ambushes](1905.md), using [hit and run tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hit_and_run_tactics).[^cite_note-PKK_tactics-164] Another change in PKK tactics was that the organisation no longer attempted to control any territory, not even after dark.[^cite_note-165] Violence increased throughout 2004 and 2005[^cite_note-security-112] during which the PKK was blamed for dozens of bombings in Western Turkey throughout 2005,[^cite_note-fas-38] including the [2005 Kuşadası minibus bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Ku%C5%9Fadas%C4%B1_minibus_bombing) (which killed five),[^cite_note-166] although the PKK denied responsibility.[^cite_note-167]

#### 2006

In March 2006 heavy fighting broke out around Diyarbakir between the PKK and Turkish security forces, as well as large riots because of "local anger over high unemployment, poverty and Ankara's reluctance to grant more autonomy to the mainly Kurdish region".[^cite_note-168] The army closed the roads to [Diyarbakır Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r_Airport) and shut down many schools and businesses.[^cite_note-security-112] In August, the [Kurdistan Freedom Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Freedom_Hawks) (TAK), which vowed to "turn Turkey into hell",[^cite_note-169] launched a major bombing campaign. On 25 August two coordinated low-level blasts targeted a bank in [Adana](185243.md), on 27 August a school in Istanbul was targeted by a bombing, on 28 August there were three coordinated attacks in [Marmaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmaris) and one in [Antalya](361527.md) targeting the tourist industry[^cite_note-security-112] and on 30 August there was a TAK bombing in [Mersin](1146241.md).[^cite_note-170] These bombings were condemned by the PKK,[^cite_note-Freedom_Falcons-42] which declared its fifth cease-fire on 1 October 2006,[^cite_note-ceasefires-98] which lessened the intensity of the conflict. Minor clashes continued due to Turkish military operations. In total, the conflict claimed over 500 lives in 2006.[^cite_note-security-112] 2006 also saw the PKK assassinate one of their former commanders, [Kani Yilmaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kani_Yilmaz), in February, in Iraq.[^cite_note-Turkishweekly-128]

#### 2007

In May 2007, there was a [bombing in Ankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Ankara_bombing) that killed 6[^cite_note-bodycount-171][^cite_note-later_death-172][^cite_note-8th_death-173][^cite_note-9th_death-174] and injured 121 people.[^cite_note-bodycount-171] The Turkish government alleged the PKK was responsible for the bombing.[^cite_note-175] On 4 June, a PKK suicide bombing in [Tunceli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunceli) killed seven soldiers and wounded six at a military base.[^cite_note-176] Tensions across the Iraqi border also started playing up as Turkish forces entered Iraq several times in pursuit of PKK fighting and In June, as 4 soldiers were killed by landmines, large areas of Iraqi Kurdistan were shelled which damaged 9 villages and forced residents to flee.[^cite_note-177] On 7 October 2007, 40–50 PKK fighters[^cite_note-PKK_tactics-164] [ambushed an 18-man Turkish commando unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2007_clashes_in_Hakkari) in the Gabar mountains, killing 15 commandos and injuring three,[^cite_note-ambush-178] which made it the deadliest PKK attack since the 1990s.[^cite_note-PKK_tactics-164] In response a law was passed allowing the Turkish military to take action inside Iraqi territory.[^cite_note-179] Then on 21 October 2007, 150–200 militants attacked an outpost, in [Dağlıca, Yüksekova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da%C4%9Fl%C4%B1ca,_Y%C3%BCksekova), manned by a 50-man infantry unit. The outpost was almost overrun and the PKK killed 12, wounded 17 and captured 6 Turkish soldiers which were released later. They then withdrew into Iraqi Kurdistan. The Turkish military killed 32 PKK fighters in hot pursuit operations, after the attack, however this was denied by the PKK.[^cite_note-PKK_tactics-164] The Turkish military responded by bombing PKK bases on 24 October, which resulted in many camps and caves being destroyed, along with 200 PKK insurgents dying in the process.[^cite_note-180] and started preparing for a major cross-border military operation.[^cite_note-ambush-178]

#### 2008

This major cross-border offensive, dubbed [Operation Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Sun), started on 21 February 2008[^cite_note-Operation_Sun-181] and was preceded by an aerial offensive against PKK camps in northern Iraq, which began on 16 December 2007.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-jpost-183] Between 3,000 and 10,000 Turkish forces took part in the offensive.[^cite_note-Operation_Sun-181] Around 230 PKK fighters were killed in the ground offensive, while 27 Turkish forces were killed. According to the PKK however, this was completely false and that over 125 Turkish forces were killed, while PKK casualties were in the tens.[^cite_note-184] Smaller scale Turkish operations against PKK bases in Iraqi Kurdistan continued afterwards.[^cite_note-185] On 27 July 2008, Turkey blamed the PKK for [an Istanbul double-bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Istanbul_bombings) which killed 17 and injured 154 people. The PKK denied any involvement.[^cite_note-186] On 4 October, the most violent clashes since the [October 2007 clashes in Hakkari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2007_clashes_in_Hakkari) erupted as the PKK attacked the [2008 Aktütün attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Akt%C3%BCt%C3%BCn_attack) post in [Şemdinli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eemdinli) in the [Hakkâri Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakk%C3%A2ri_Province), at night. 17 Turkish soldiers were killed and 20 were injured, meanwhile 123 PKK fighters were killed during the fighting.[^cite_note-Oct08-187] On 10 November, the Iranian Kurdish insurgent group [PJAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PJAK) declared it would be halting operations inside Iran to start fighting the Turkish military.[^cite_note-todayonline.com-188]

#### 2009

At the start of 2009 Turkey opened its first Kurdish-language TV-channel, [TRT 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TRT_6),[^cite_note-189] and on 19 March 2009 [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Turkish_local_elections) were held in Turkey in which the pro-Kurdish [Democratic Society Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Society_Party) (DTP) won a majority of the vote in the South East. Soon after, on 13 April 2009, the [PKK declared its sixth ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009%E2%80%932010_Kurdistan_Workers'_Party_ceasefire), after Abdullah Öcalan called on them to end military operations and prepare for peace.[^cite_note-ceasefires-98] The following day the Turkish authorities arrested 53 Kurdish politicians of the [Democratic Society Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Society_Party) (DTP).[^cite_note-190] In September Turkey's [Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erdo%C4%9Fan)-government launched the [Kurdish initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_initiative#Kurdish_initiative), which included plans to rename Kurdish villages that had been given Turkish names, expand the scope of the freedom of expression, restore Turkish citizenship to Kurdish refugees, strengthen local governments, and extend a partial amnesty for PKK fighters.[^cite_note-191] But the plans for the Kurdish initiative where heavily hurt after the DTP was banned by the Turkish constitutional court[^cite_note-initiative-192] on 11 December 2009 and its leaders were subsequently put on trial for terrorism.[^cite_note-dtp-193] A total of 1,400 DTP members were arrested and 900 detained in the government crackdown against the party.[^cite_note-194] This caused major riots by Kurds all over Turkey and resulted in violent clashes between pro-Kurdish and security forces as well as pro-Turkish demonstrators, which resulted in several injuries and fatalities.[^cite_note-initiative-192] On 7 December the PKK launched [an ambush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resadiye_shooting) in [Reşadiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re%C5%9Fadiye) which killed seven and injured three Turkish soldiers, which became the deadliest PKK attack in that region since the 1990s.[^cite_note-195]

#### 2010

On 1 May 2010 the PKK declared an end to its cease-fire,[^cite_note-196] launching an attack in Tunceli that killed four and injured seven soldiers.[^cite_note-197] On 31 May, Abdullah Öcalan declared an end to his attempts at re-approachment and establishing dialogue with the Turkish government, leaving PKK top commanders in charge of the conflict. The PKK then stepped up its armed activities,[^cite_note-198] starting with a missile attack on a navy base in [İskenderun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0skenderun), killing 7 and wounding 6 soldiers.[^cite_note-199] On 18 and 19 June, heavy fighting broke out that resulted in the death of 12 PKK fighters, 12 Turkish soldiers and injury of 17 Turkish soldiers, as the PKK launched three separate attacks in Hakkari and Elazig provinces.[^cite_note-worldbulletin.net-200][^cite_note-201]

Another major attack in Hakkari occurred on 20 July 2010, killing six and wounding seventeen Turkish soldiers, with one PKK fighter being killed.[^cite_note-http-202] The next day, Murat Karayilan, the leader of the PKK, announced that the PKK would lay down its arms if the Kurdish issue would be resolved through dialogue and threatened to declare independence if this demand was not met.[^cite_note-World_Bulletin-203] Turkish forces had killed 187 and captured 160 PKK members by 14 July, and killed another 227 by the end of the year.[^cite_note-worldbulletin-204] By 27 July, Turkish news sources reported the deaths of 72 security forces, which exceeded the 2009 toll. On 12 August, however, a [ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan) cease-fire was declared by the PKK. In November the cease-fire was extended until the [Turkish general election on 12 June 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Turkish_general_election), despite alleging that Turkey had launched over 80 military operations against them during this period.[^cite_note-ceasefires-98] Despite the truce, the PKK responded to these military operations by launching retaliatory attacks in Siirt and Hakkari provinces, killing 12 Turkish soldiers.[^cite_note-205]

#### 2011



A demonstration against the PKK in [Frankfurt](10992.md), October 2011

The cease-fire was revoked early, on 28 February 2011.[^cite_note-Champion-206] Soon afterwards three PKK fighters were killed while trying to get into Turkey through northern Iraq.[^cite_note-hurriyetdailynews-207] In May, counter-insurgency operations left 12 PKK fighters and 5 soldiers dead. This then resulted in major [Kurdish protests across Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Kurdish_protests_in_Turkey) as part of a civil disobedience campaign launched by the pro-Kurdish [Peace and Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_and_Democracy_Party) (BDP),[^cite_note-bianet.org-208] during these protests 2 people were killed, 308 injured and 2,506 arrested by Turkish authorities.[^cite_note-209] The 12 June elections saw a historical performance for the pro-Kurdish [Peace and Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_and_Democracy_Party) (BDP) which won 36 seats in the South-East, which was more than the ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP), which won only 30 seats in Kurdish areas.[^cite_note-todayszaman.com-210] However, six of the 36 elected BDP deputies remain in Turkish jails as of June 2011.[^cite_note-reuters.com-211] One of the six jailed deputies, Hatip Dicle, was then stripped of his elected position by the constitutional court, after which the 30 free MPs declared a boycott of Turkish parliament.[^cite_note-todayszaman-212] The PKK intensified its campaign again, in July killing 20 Turkish soldiers in two weeks, during which at least 10 PKK fighters were killed, the most of these occurring in a [single ambush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvan_ambush).[^cite_note-arabnews.com-213] On 17 August 2011, the Turkish Armed Forces launched [multiple raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_2011_Turkey-Iraq_cross-border_raid) against Kurdish rebels, striking 132 targets.[^cite_note-reuters-214] Turkish military bombed PKK targets in northern Iraq in six days of air raids, according to General Staff, where 90–100 PKK Soldiers were killed, and at least 80 injured.[^cite_note-jpost.com-215] From July to September Iran carried out [an offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Iran-Iraq_cross-border_raids) against the PJAK in Northern Iraq, which resulted in a cease-fire on 29 September. After the cease-fire the PJAK withdrew its forces from Iran and joined with the PKK to fight Turkey. Turkish counter-terrorism operations reported a sharp increase of Iranian citizens among the insurgents killed in October and November, such as the six PJAK fighters killed in Çukurca on 28 October.[^cite_note-jamestown.org-216] On 19 October, twenty-six Turkish soldiers were killed[^cite_note-zee-217] and 18 injured[^cite_note-kuna.net.kw-218] in 8 simultaneous PKK attacks in Cukurca and Yuksekova, in Hakkari provieen 10,000 and 15,000 full-time, which is the highest it has ever been.[^cite_note-219]

#### 2012

In summer 2012, the conflict with the PKK took a violent curve, in parallel with the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md)[^cite_note-220] as President [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) ceded control of several Kurdish cities in Syria to the [PYD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Union_Party_\(Syria\)), the Syrian affiliate of the PKK, and Turkey armed ISIS and other Islamic groups against Kurds.[^cite_note-221] Turkish foreign minister [Ahmet Davutoglu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmet_Davutoglu) accused the Assad government of arming the group.[^cite_note-222] In June and August there were [heavy clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June%E2%80%93August_2012_Hakkari_clashes) in Hakkari province, described as the most violent in years.[^cite_note-turkishweekly2-223] as the PKK attempted to seize control of [Şemdinli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eemdinli) and engage the Turkish army in a "frontal battle" by blocking the roads leading to the town from Iran and Iraq and setting up [DShK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DShK) heavy machine guns and rocket launchers on high ground to ambush Turkish motorized units that would be sent to re-take the town. However the Turkish army avoided the trap by destroying the heavy weapons from the air and using long range artillery to root out the PKK. The Turkish military declared operation was ended successfully on 11 August, claiming to have killed 115 guerrillas and lost only six soldiers and two village guards.[^cite_note-224] On 20 August, eight people were killed and 66 wounded by a [deadly bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Gaziantep_bombing) in [Gaziantep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaziantep).[^cite_note-225] According to the [KCK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koma_Civak%C3%AAn_Kurdistan) 400 incidents of shelling, air bombardment and armed clashes occurred in August.[^cite_note-crisis-69]

### Peace process 2012–2015

Main article: [Kurdish–Turkish peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish%E2%80%93Turkish_peace_process)

On 28 December 2012, in a television interview upon a question of whether the government had a project to solve the issue, [Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) said that the government was conducting negotiations with jailed rebel leader [Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_%C3%96calan).[^cite_note-Yes,_we_do.-226] Negotiations were initially named as *Solution Process* (Çözüm Süreci) in public. While negotiations were going on, there were numerous events that were regarded as sabotage to derail the talks: The [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_murder_of_Kurdish_activists_in_Paris) of the PKK administrators [Sakine Cansız](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakine_Cans%C4%B1z), [Fidan Doğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidan_Do%C4%9Fan) and [Leyla Söylemez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leyla_S%C3%B6ylemez) in Paris,[^cite_note-227] revealing Öcalan's talks with the pro-Kurdish party [Peoples' Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples'_Democratic_Party_\(Turkey\)) (HDP) to the public via the *[Milliyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milliyet)* newspaper[^cite_note-Breaking_the_crystal-228] and finally, the bombings of the Justice Ministry of Turkey and Erdoğan's office at the Ak Party headquarters in Ankara.[^cite_note-Twin_bombs_strike_the_peace-229] However, both parties vehemently condemned all three events as they occurred and stated that they were determined anyway. Finally on 21 March 2013, after months of negotiations with the Turkish Government, Abdullah Ocalan's letter to people was read both in Turkish and Kurdish during [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz) celebrations in [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r). The letter called a cease-fire that included disarmament and withdrawal from Turkish soil and calling *an end to armed struggle*. [PKK](69680.md) announced that they would obey, stating that the year of 2013 is the year of solution either through war or through peace. [Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) welcomed the letter stating that concrete steps will follow PKK's withdrawal.[^cite_note-Ceasefire_and_peace-101]



Kurdish PKK guerilla at the [Newroz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newroz_as_celebrated_by_Kurds) celebration in kandil, 23 March 2014

On 25 April 2013, PKK announced that it would be withdrawing all its forces within [Turkey](11125639.md) to northern [Iraq](7515928.md).[^cite_note-Withdrawel-230] According to the Turkish government[^cite_note-231] and the Kurds[^cite_note-232] and most of the press,[^cite_note-233] this move marks the end of 30-year-old conflict. Second phase which includes constitutional and legal changes towards the recognition of human rights of the Kurds starts simultaneously with withdrawal.

#### Escalation

Main article: [2014 Kobanî protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Koban%C3%AE_protests)

On 6 and 7 October 2014, riots erupted in various cities in Turkey for protesting the Siege of Kobane. The Kurds accused the Turkish government of supporting ISIS and not letting people send support for Kobane Kurds. Protesters were met with tear gas and water cannons. 37 people were killed in protests.[^cite_note-234] During these protests, there were deadly clashes between PKK and Hizbullah sympathizers.[^cite_note-235] 3 soldiers were killed by PKK in January 2015,[^cite_note-236] as a sign of rising tensions in the country.

### 2015 to February 2025

Main article: [Timeline of the Kurdistan Workers' Party insurgency (2015–2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kurdistan_Workers'_Party_insurgency_\(2015%E2%80%932025\))

See also: [Turkish involvement in the Syrian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_involvement_in_the_Syrian_Civil_War) and [Operation Olive Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Olive_Branch)

In June 2015, the main [Syrian Kurdish](3821855.md) militia, [YPG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Protection_Units), and the Turkey's main pro-Kurdish party, HDP, accused Turkey of allowing [Islamic State](9087364.md) (ISIL) soldiers to cross its border and attack the Kurdish city of [Kobanî](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koban%C3%AE) in Syria.[^cite_note-237] The conflict between Turkey and PKK escalated following the 20 July [2015 Suruç bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Suru%C3%A7_bombing) attack on progressive activists, which was claimed by ISIL. During the 24–25 July 2015 [Operation Martyr Yalçın](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Martyr_Yal%C3%A7%C4%B1n), Turkey bombed alleged PKK bases in Iraq and [PYD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Union_Party_\(Syria\)) bases in Syria's Kurdish region [Rojava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Administration_of_North_and_East_Syria), purportedly retaliating the killing of two policeman in the town of [Ceylanpınar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceylanp%C4%B1nar) (which the PKK denied carrying out) and effectively ending the cease-fire (after many months of increasing tensions).[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] Turkish warplanes also bombed [YPG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Protection_Units) bases in [Syria](7515849.md).[^cite_note-241]



Turkish police announcing seizure of PKK ammunition in [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r), August 2015

Violence soon spread throughout Turkey. Many Kurdish businesses were destroyed by mobs.[^cite_note-242] The headquarters and branches of the pro-Kurdish [Peoples' Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples'_Democratic_Party_\(Turkey\)) (HDP) were also attacked.[^cite_note-243] There are reports of civilians being killed in several Kurdish-populated towns and villages.[^cite_note-244] The [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) raised their concerns over the attacks on civilians and the 4 September 2015 blockade of Cizre.[^cite_note-245]

But also the Kurdish rebel fighters did not sit still: a Turkish Governor claimed that Kurdish assailants had fired on a police vehicle in [Adana](185243.md) in September 2015, killing two officers, and some unspecified "clash" with PKK rebels purportedly took place in [Hakkâri Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakk%C3%A2ri_Province). President Erdogan claimed that between 23 July and late September, 150 Turkish officers and 2,000 Kurdish rebels had been killed.[^cite_note-246] In December 2015, Turkish military operations in the Kurdish regions of southeastern Turkey had killed hundreds of civilians, displaced hundreds of thousands and caused massive destruction in residential areas.[^cite_note-247] According to the [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW) report, "Local human rights groups have recorded well over 100 civilian deaths and multiple injuries."[^cite_note-248]

The spring of 2016 saw the seasonal uptick in combat activity. In May, a Turkish [Bell AH-1 SuperCobra](574557.md) helicopter was documented shot down by a PKK-fired Russian made [MANPADS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MANPADS).[^cite_note-249]

On 6 May 2016, [HBDH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples'_United_Revolutionary_Movement), an umbrella organization built around the Kurdish [PKK](69680.md), attacked a [Gendarmerie General Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie_General_Command) base in [Giresun Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giresun_Province) in northeastern Turkey. According to news reports, a roadside bomb exploded, targeting a Gendarmerie vehicle.[^cite_note-\:2-250] HDBH claimed responsibility for the attack on 8 May, stating that three gendarmes died in the attack, as well as the Base Commander, who was the intended target.[^cite_note-\:3-251] the Joint Command of the [HBDH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples'_United_Revolutionary_Movement) has claimed responsibility for several more attacks in the region, primarily targeting Turkish soldiers or gendarmes. The tactics employed by the alliance are very similar to those used by the PKK. The most notable attack came on 19 July 2016, just 4 days after the [2016 Turkish coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt). HBDH reported that they had killed 11 Turkish riot police in [Trabzon Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trabzon_Province) at 08\:30 that morning.[^cite_note-\:4-252] The HBDH report is consistent in time and location to an attack reported by Doğan News Agency, in which "unknown assailants" fired on a police checkpoint. This report states that 3 officers were killed and 5 were injured, along with a civilian.[^cite_note-\:5-253]

In January 2018, the Turkish military and its [Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish-backed_Free_Syrian_Army) and [Sham Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sham_Legion) allies began a [cross-border operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_military_operation_in_Afrin) in the Kurdish-majority [Afrin Canton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrin_Canton) in Northern Syria, against the Kurdish-led [Democratic Union Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Union_Party_\(Syria\)) in Syria (PYD) and the U.S.-supported [YPG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Protection_Units) Kurdish militia.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255] In March 2018, Turkey launched [military operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tigris_Shield) to eliminate the Kurdish PKK fighters in northern [Iraq](7515928.md).[^cite_note-256] This failed however, as the PKK has expanded its operations in Iraq.[^cite_note-257]



[Women's Protection Units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Protection_Units) (YPJ) fighters in the [Afrin Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrin_Region) during the Turkish operation in 2018

In October 2019, the Turkish force launched an operation against Syrian Kurds in the Northern Syria which has been termed [Operation Peace Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Peace_Spring).[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259]

On 14 February 2021, Turkish Minister of Defense [Hulusi Akar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulusi_Akar) claimed that 13 soldiers and police officers, who had been held hostage by the PKK since 2015 and 2016, were executed during an attempted rescue operation.[^cite_note-260] Erdoğan blamed the United States and Kurdish politicians for the failed operation,[^cite_note-nyhostages-261] while [CHP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_People's_Party_\(Turkey\)) leader [Kılıçdaroğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_K%C4%B1l%C4%B1%C3%A7daro%C4%9Flu) accused Erdoğan of being responsible for the deaths.[^cite_note-262] The PKK claimed the hostages were killed by Turkish airstrikes during the operation.[^cite_note-nyhostages-261][^cite_note-263] The father of one deceased hostage Semih Özbey was summoned to identify his son, and according to the [Turkish Human Rights Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Association_\(Turkey\)) president, stated he saw a bullet wound in his son's head.[^cite_note-264] In an interview with [Sözcü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B6zc%C3%BC), the father noted he believed the hostages were executed, but was only shown a picture of his son's face and was refused seeing his body. He added that during his sons imprisonment he spoke repeatedly to both HDP MPs and Erdoğan to no avail.[^cite_note-265] The Turkish Human Rights Association, which had previously helped return hostages from the PKK, stated their offers to help negotiate were rejected by state officials.[^cite_note-266]

On 17 April 2022, [Operation Claw-Lock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Claw-Lock) began involving a cross border offensive operation into [Iraqi Kurdistan](679693.md) allegedly (PKK claim) with the support of the [KDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Democratic_Party) and targeted areas including Metina, Zap and Avashin. The operation is currently ongoing.

In May 2022, while calls for Finland and Sweden were made to join the NATO alliance, Turkey opposed their adhesion unless these countries crack down on local Kurdish and Gulenist networks. The move has been commented as a political card to distract from ongoing economic crisis in Turkey and better engage in upcoming elections, taking an aggressive posture against foreign powers and Kurds being favorable to AKP.[^cite_note-267] On 28 June, the first day of the [2022 NATO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Madrid_summit) in Madrid, the Turkish delegation dropped their opposition to Finland and Sweden's NATO membership applications and signed a tripartite memorandum addressing Turkey's concerns regarding arms exports and the Kurdish–Turkish conflict.[^cite_note-268] On 30 June 2022, Turkish President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) said that Sweden had made a "promise" to extradite "73 terrorists" wanted by Turkey.[^cite_note-269]

On 13 November 2022, a [bombing in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Istanbul_bombing) killed six people. Turkey says the bomb was planted by a Kurdish Syrian separatist whom they arrested. On 20 November, Turkey launched [Operation Claw-Sword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Claw-Sword) – airstrikes on PKK positions in Iraq and Syrian Democratic Forces positions in Syria.

In October 2024, the PKK killed 7 people and injured 22 others in the [Turkish Aerospace Industries headquarters attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Aerospace_Industries_headquarters_attack).

## Dissolution

On 27 February 2025 Abdullah Öcalan called for an end to the over 40 year conflict, with a [disarmament](57979.md) and disbanding of the PKK.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] His statement was read out by the pro-Kurdish [DEM party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples'_Equality_and_Democracy_Party), whose lawmakers had liased between him and the government.[^cite_note-273] On 1 March 2025 the PKK executive committee, based in northern Iraq,[^cite_note-274] declared an immediate ceasefire with Turkey.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] Vice President [Cevdet Yılmaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cevdet_Y%C4%B1lmaz) said: "A new phase has been entered towards the goal of a terror-free Turkiye. ... We hope that this opportunity will be seized, this effort will be concluded quickly and successfully,".[^cite_note-277] The PKK asked for Öcalan to be released but gave no timeline for disarming and disbanding. Analysts said that if the ceasefire holds it could have consequences beyond Turkey.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] The [Syrian Democratic Forces](48205248.md) (SDF), which Turkey says is tied to the PKK, welcomed the ceasefire but said it did not apply to them.[^cite_note-280]

The PKK released a statement announcing its dissolution on 12 May 2025. The group stated they had come to a point where their demands could be met via "democratic politics"; additionally, they cited the situation in the Middle East.[^cite_note-281] The details of the PKK's disarmament remain unclear,[^cite_note-DW-282][^cite_note-CNN2025-283] and the statement did not specify if regional affiliates are affected as well.[^cite_note-CNN2025-283] However, Turkey's military will continue operations against the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) in regions where it remains active, despite the group's announcement of its dissolution.[^cite_note-theinsightinternational.com-107][^cite_note-The_Hindu-108][^cite_note-thenewregion.com-109][^cite_note-rudaw\.net-110] [Al Jazeera](30204052.md) reported that the SDF "is unlikely to follow the PKK's lead and disarm."[^cite_note-284]

Erdoğan hailed the announcement as a victory for his "Terror-Free Turkey process", with the spokesperson for his [Justice and Development Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\)) stating that a "new era" could begin.[^cite_note-CNN2025-283] Political analyst Seren Selvin Korkmaz described the disbandment as a turning point for Turkish politics.[^cite_note-DW-282] The [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace) described reactions within Turkey as "mixed", with pro-government groups crediting Turkey for the dissolution, while certain Kurdish people remained skeptical about the peace process.[^cite_note-285] Iraq and Canada issued statements welcoming the PKK's dissolution.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

## Serhildan

Main article: [Serhildan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serhildan)

The Serhildan, or people's uprising,[^cite_note-serhildan-288] started on 14 March 1990, Nusaybin during the funeral of[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] 20-year-old PKK fighter [Kamuran Dundar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamuran_Dundar?action=edit\&redlink=1), who along with 13 other fighters was killed by the Turkish military after crossing into Turkey via Syria several days earlier. Dundar came from a Kurdish nationalist family which claimed his body and held a funeral for him in Nusaybin in which he was brought to the city's main mosque and 5000 people held a march. On the way back the march turned violent and protesters clashed with the police, during which both sides fired upon each other and many people were injured. A curfew was then placed in Nusaybin, tanks and special forces were brought in and[^cite_note-serhildan-288] some 700 people were arrested.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] Riots spread to nearby towns[^cite_note-serhildan-288] and in [Cizre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cizre) over 15,000 people, constituting about half the town's population took part in riots in which five people were killed, 80 injured and 155 arrested.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] Widespread riots took place throughout the Southeast on [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz), the Kurdish new-year celebrations, which at the time were banned.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] Protests slowed down over the next two weeks as many started to stay home and Turkish forces were ordered not to intervene unless absolutely necessarily[^cite_note-serhildan-288] but factory sit-ins, go-slows, work boycotts and "unauthorized" strikes were still held despite being banned.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289]

Protests are often held on 21 March, or [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz).[^cite_note-ifex-290] Most notably in 1992, when thousands of protesters clashed with security forces all over the country and where the army allegedly disobeyed an order from President [Suleyman Demirel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleyman_Demirel) not to attack the protest.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] In the heavy violence that ensued during that year's Nowroz protest some 55[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] people were killed, mainly in Şırnak (26 killed), Cizre (29 killed) and Nusaybin (14 killed) and it included a police officer and a soldier. Over 200 people were injured[^cite_note-metu-291] and another 200 were arrested.[^cite_note-asylumlaw-289] According to Governor of Şırnak, Mustafa Malay, the violence was caused by 500 to 1,500 armed rebels which he alleged, entered the town during the festival. However, he conceded that "the security forces did not establish their targets properly and caused great damage to civilian houses."[^cite_note-292]

Since [Abdullah Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_%C3%96calan)'s capture on 15 February 1999, protests are also held every year on that date.[^cite_note-ifex-290]

## Casualties



Funeral of a baby killed in the [Şırnak clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C4%B1rnak_clashes_\(2015%E2%80%932016\)), 2015

According to figures released by the state-run [Anadolu Agency](43356543.md), citing a Turkish security source, from the start of the conflict in 1984 until mid-2015 there were approximately 36,345 deaths. The PKK was responsible for the deaths of 6,741 civilians (with another 14,257 civilians wounded). A total of 7,230 members of the Turkish security forces (including soldiers, police and village guards) were killed and 21,128 were wounded. Turkish security forces in turn killed 22,374 PKK militants (with approximately 1,480 militants wounded or captured injured). Over the following 31 years PKK carried out some 83,500 attacks.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294] Among the civilian casualties, till 2012, 157 were teachers.[^cite_note-295] From August 1984 to June 2007, a total of 13,327 soldiers and 7,620 civilians were said to have been wounded.[^cite_note-wounded-296] About 2,500 people were said to have been killed between 1984 and 1991, while over 17,500 were killed between 1991 and 1995.[^cite_note-casualty_years-297] The number of murders committed by Village Guards from 1985 to 1996 is put at 296 by official estimates.[^cite_note-298]

Contrary to the newest estimate, earlier figures by the Turkish military put the number of PKK casualties much higher, with 26,128 PKK dead by June 2007,[^cite_note-wounded-296] and 29,704 by March 2009. Between the start of the second insurgency in 2004, and March 2009, 2,462 PKK militants were claimed killed.[^cite_note-Turkish_casualties-161] However, later figures provided by the military for the 1984–2012 period, revised down the number of killed PKK members to 21,800.[^cite_note-299]

Both the [PKK](69680.md) and [Turkish military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_military) have accused each other of civilian deaths. Since the 1970s, the European Court of Human Rights has condemned Turkey for the thousands of human rights abuses against Kurdish people.[^cite_note-ETCHR1-81][^cite_note-AnnualETCHR-82] The judgments are related to systematic executions of Kurdish civilians,[^cite_note-hum1-83] torturing,[^cite_note-tort1-300] forced displacements,[^cite_note-301] thousands of destroyed villages,[^cite_note-St._Martin's_Press-86][^cite_note-Univ_Of_Texas_Press-87][^cite_note-Taylor_and_Francis-88] arbitrary arrests,[^cite_note-autogenerated1-89] murdered and disappeared Kurdish journalists, politicians and activists.[^cite_note-khrp.org-90] Turkey has been also condemned for killing Kurdish civilians and blaming the PKK in the ECHR.[^cite_note-hum1-83]

According to the *Los Angeles Times*, 4,000 villages have been destroyed since the beginning of the uprising,[^cite_note-LA_Times-302] in which U.S. Government, who supports the PKK, accused the Turkish Military for the forced evacuation of between 380,000 and 1,000,000 Kurdish villagers from their homes [^cite_note-findlaw1-303] According to the [Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times) some 5,000 Turkish civilians and 35,000 Kurdish civilians have been killed, 17,000 Kurdish civilians have disappeared and 119,000 Kurdish civilians have been imprisoned as a result of the conflict.[^cite_note-LA_Times-302] According to the [Humanitarian Law Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_Law_Project), 2,400 Kurdish villages were destroyed and 18,000 Kurds were executed.[^cite_note-findlaw1-303][^cite_note-executions-304] In total up to 3,000,000 people (mainly Kurds) have been displaced by the conflict,[^cite_note-displaced-305] an estimated 1,000,000 of which are still internally displaced as of 2009.[^cite_note-306] The Assyrian Minority was heavily affected as well, as now most (50–60 thousand/70,000) of its population is in refuge in Europe.

[Sebahat Tuncel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebahat_Tuncel), an elected MP from the BDP, put the PKK's casualties at 18,000 as of July 2011.[^cite_note-sebahat-307]
