Turkey strategic objective was to prevent [Afrin canton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrin_canton) from connecting with [YPG Manbij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahba_Canton) and other [Rojava regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_the_Democratic_Federation_of_Northern_Syria). Accordingly, only a minor part of these operations casualties were from Turkey forces vs YPG forces.

## Demographic effect

Further information: [Kurdish villages depopulated by Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_villages_depopulated_by_Turkey)

The [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) of predominantly Kurdish areas in Turkey's East and South-East were also bound in the early ideas and policies of modern Turkish nationalism, going back to as early as 1918 to the manifesto of Turkish nationalist [Ziya Gökalp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziya_G%C3%B6kalp) "Turkification, Islamization and Modernization".[^cite_note-joost-330] The evolving [Young Turk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turks) conscience adopted a specific interpretation of progressism, a trend of thought which emphasizes the human ability to make, improve and reshape human society, relying on science, technology and experimentation.[^cite_note-\:7-331] This notion of social evolution was used to support and justify policies of population control.[^cite_note-\:7-331] The [Kurdish rebellions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_rebellions_in_Turkey) provided a comfortable pretext for Turkish [Kemalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemalism) to implement such ideas, and in a [Settlement Law was issued in 1934](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1934_Turkish_Resettlement_Law). It created a complex pattern of interaction between state of society, in which the regime favored its people in a distant geography, populated by locals marked as hostile.[^cite_note-\:7-331]

During the 1990s, a predominantly Kurdish-dominated Eastern and South-Eastern Turkey (Kurdistan) was depopulated due to the Kurdish–Turkish conflict.[^cite_note-joost-330] Turkey depopulated and destroyed rural settlements on a large scale, resulting in massive resettlement of a rural Kurdish population in urban areas and leading to development and re-design of population settlement schemes across the countryside.[^cite_note-joost-330] According to Dr. Joost Jongerden, Turkish settlement and re-settlement policies during the 1990s period were influenced by two different forces – the desire to expand administration to rural areas and an alternative view of urbanization, allegedly producing "Turkishness".[^cite_note-joost-330]

## Human rights abuses

Both Turkey and the PKK have committed numerous [human rights](13831.md) [abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuses) during the conflict. Former French ambassador to Turkey [Eric Rouleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Rouleau) states:[^cite_note-rouleau-332]

> According to the [Ministry of Justice](10283737.md), in addition to the 35,000 people killed in military campaigns, 17,500 were assassinated between 1984, when the conflict began, and 1998. An additional 1,000 people were reportedly assassinated in the first nine months of 1999. According to the Turkish press, the authors of these crimes, none of whom have been arrested, belong to groups of mercenaries working either directly or indirectly for the security agencies.

### Abuses by the Turkish side

Main article: [Turkish war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_war_crimes)

Further information: [Human rights of Kurdish people in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_of_Kurdish_people_in_Turkey)

Since the 1970s, the European Court of Human Rights has condemned Turkey for thousands of human rights abuses against Kurdish people.[^cite_note-ETCHR1-81][^cite_note-AnnualETCHR-82] The judgments are related to systematic executions of Kurdish civilians,[^cite_note-hum1-83] forced recruitments,[^cite_note-hum1-83] torturing,[^cite_note-tort1-300][^cite_note-\:1-84] forced displacements,[^cite_note-ford1-333] thousands of destroyed villages,[^cite_note-vilg2-334] arbitrary arrests,[^cite_note-335] murdered and disappeared Kurdish journalists.[^cite_note-kjour1-336] The latest judgments are from 2014.[^cite_note-hum1-83] According to David L. Philips, more than 1,500 people affiliated with the Kurdish opposition parties and organizations were murdered by unidentified assailants between 1986 and 1996. The government-backed mercenaries assassinated hundreds of suspected PKK sympathizers.[^cite_note-davidp1-122] The Turkish government is held responsible by Turkish human rights organizations for at least 3,438 civilian deaths in the conflict between 1987 and 2000.[^cite_note-belge-337]



[Hakkari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Hakkari_Assault), 2016

#### Massacres

In November 1992, Turkish gendarmerie officers forced the leader of Kelekçi village to evacuate all of the inhabitants, before shooting at them and their houses with heavy weapons. The soldiers set fire to nine houses and forced all villagers to flee. Later soldiers burned the rest of the village and destroyed all 136 houses.[^cite_note-338]

In 1993, Mehmet Ogut, his pregnant wife and all their seven children were burned to death by [Turkish special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Forces_\(Turkish_Armed_Forces\)) soldiers. The Turkish authorities initially blamed the PKK and refused to investigate the case until it was reopened in 2010. The investigations concluded in late 2014 with sentences of life imprisonment for three gendarme officers, a member of the special forces and nine soldiers.[^cite_note-339]

In August 1993, Turkish security forces opened fire during a protest, killing 10 people and wounding 51.[^cite_note-340]

On 8 September 1993, the [Turkish Air Force](1799517.md) dropped a bomb near the Munzur mountains, killing two women. In the same year, Turkish security forces attacked the town of [Lice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lice,_Turkey), destroying 401 houses, 242 shops and massacring more than 30 civilians, and leaving 100 wounded.[^cite_note-licebook-341]

On 26 March 1994 Turkish F-16 jets and a helicopter circled two villages and bombed them, killing 38 Kurdish civilians.[^cite_note-hum1-83] The Turkish authorities blamed the PKK and took pictures of the dead children to release in the press. The European Court of Human Rights ordered Turkey to pay 2.3 million euros to the families of victims.[^cite_note-hum1-83] The event is known as the [Kuşkonar massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku%C5%9Fkonar_massacre).

In 1995, a 52-year-old woman claimed that Turkish soldiers killed her husband and her husband's brother and took pictures of their corpses with weapons. She said that the victims were laughed upon as PKK members.[^cite_note-342]

In 1995, *[The European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_European_\(newspaper\))* newspaper published in its front-page pictures of Turkish soldiers who posed for a camera with the severed heads of PKK fighters. Kurdish fighters were beheaded by Turkish special forces soldiers.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

In late March 2006, Turkish security forces who tried to prevent the funerals of PKK fighters clashed with demonstrators, killing at least eight Kurdish protesters, including four children under the age of 10.[^cite_note-refworld1hrw-345]



[Cizre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cizre), 2016

In August 2015, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) reported that Turkish government airstrikes killed eight residents and injured at least eight others – including a child – in an attack on the village of Zergele, in the Kandil Mountains in the Kurdistan Region of Iraq.[^cite_note-346]

On 21 January 2016, a report published by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) stated that more than 150 civilians had been killed in Cizre. According to Amnesty International, the curfews had been imposed in more than 19 different towns and districts, putting the lives of hundreds of thousands of people at risk. Additionally, the report stated that the government's disproportionate restrictions on movement and other arbitrary measures were resembling [collective punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_punishment), a war crime under the 1949 Geneva Conventions.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348]

In 2019, Turkish soldiers killed 11 Kurdish civilians, eight of them children in artillery attack.[^cite_note-349]

[Human Rights Watch](46530.md) noted in 1992 that:

- Turkish government forces have, during the conflict with the PKK, also committed serious violations of international human rights and humanitarian law, including [torture](47702.md), extrajudicial killings, and indiscriminate fire. We continue to demand that the Turkish government investigate and hold accountable those members of its security forces responsible for these violations. Nonetheless, under international law, the government abuses cannot under any circumstances be seen to justify or excuse those committed by Ocalan's PKK.

  [^cite_note-hrwdalema-350]

- The Kurdish Workers Party (PKK), a separatist group that espouses the use of violence for political ends, continues to wage guerrilla warfare in the southeast, frequently in violation of international humanitarian law, or the laws of war. Instead of attempting to capture, question and indict people suspected of illegal activity, Turkish security forces killed suspects in house raids, thus acting as investigator, judge, jury and executioner. Police routinely asserted that such deaths occurred in shoot-outs between police and "terrorists". In many cases, eyewitnesses reported that no firing came from the attacked house or apartment. Reliable reports indicated that while the occupants of raided premises were shot and killed, no police were killed or wounded during the raids. This discrepancy suggests that the killings were summary, extrajudicial executions, in violation of international human rights and humanitarian law.

  [^cite_note-hrwdevelopments-351]

Turkish–Kurdish human rights activists in Germany accused Turkey of using chemical weapons against PKK. Hans Baumann, a German expert on photo forgeries, investigated the authenticity of the photos and claimed that the photos were authentic. A forensics report released by the Hamburg University Hospital has backed the allegations. Claudia Roth from Germany's Green Party demanded an explanation from the Turkish government.[^cite_note-spiegel-352] The Turkish Foreign Ministry spokesman Selçuk Ünal commented on the issue. He said that he did not need to emphasize that the accusations were groundless. He added that Turkey signed to the Chemical Weapons Convention in 1997, and Turkey did not possess chemical weapons.[^cite_note-353]

In response to the activities of the PKK, the Turkish government placed Southeastern Anatolia, where citizens of Kurdish descent are in the majority, under military rule. The Turkish Army and the Kurdish village guards loyal to it have abused Kurdish civilians, resulting in [mass migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_migration) to cities.[^cite_note-mcdowall-354] The Government claimed that the displacement policy aimed to remove the shelter and support of the local population and consequently, the population of cities such as Diyarbakır and [Cizre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cizre) more than doubled.[^cite_note-phillips-355] However, martial law and military rule was lifted in the last provinces in 2002.

#### State terrorism

Since its foundation, the [Republic of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_turkey) has pursued variously [assimilationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assimilationist) and [repressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression) policies towards the [Kurdish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_people).[^cite_note-356] At the beginning of the conflict, the PKK's relationship with its civilian supporters created incentives for the Turkish government to use [terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_terrorism) against the Kurdish citizens in the Kurdish dominated southeast region of Turkey.[^cite_note-civtar1-126] Since the early 1980s, the authorities have systematically used arbitrary arrests, executions of suspects, excessive force, and torture to suppress the opponents. In 1993, the report published by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated:[^cite_note-hrwrep1993-357]

> Kurds in Turkey have been killed, tortured and disappeared at an appalling rate since the coalition government of Prime Minister Suleyman Demirel took office in November 1991. In addition, many of their cities have been brutally attacked by security forces, hundreds of their villages have been forcibly evacuated, their ethnic identity continues to be attacked, their rights to free expression denied and their political freedom placed in jeopardy.

According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), the authorities even executed the Kurdish civilians and took the pictures of their corpses with the weapons, they carried for staging the events, in order to show them as [Kurdistan Workers Party](69680.md) (PKK) "terrorists" to press. In 1995, another report published by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated:[^cite_note-358]

> Based on B.G.'s statement and substantial additional evidence, Human Rights Watch believes that the official government casualty estimates severely misrepresent the true number of civilians slain by government forces. It is likely that many of the persons referred to in the official estimates as "PKK casualties" were in fact civilians shot by mistake or deliberately killed by security forces. Witness testimony also demonstrates that many of the Turkish government's denials of wrong-doing by the Turkish security forces are fabrications manufactured by soldiers or officials somewhere along the government's chain of command.

Shooting and killing peaceful demonstrators was one of the methods the security forces used to spread fear. In 1992, the security forces killed more than 103 demonstrators, 93 of them during the celebration of Newroz in three Kurdish cities. No security force member was ever charged with any of the deaths.[^cite_note-hrwrep1993-357]

In the early 1990s, hundreds of people had disappeared after they had been taken into custody by security forces. Only in 1992, more than 450 people had been reportedly killed. Among those killed were journalists, teachers, doctors, human rights activists and political leaders. The security forces usually denied having detained the victims but sometimes they claimed that they had released the victims after "holding them briefly".[^cite_note-hrwrep1993-357] According to the [Human Rights Association (İHD)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Association_\(Turkey\)), there have been 940 cases of enforced disappearance since the 1990s. In addition to that, more than 3,248 people who were murdered in extrajudicial killings are believed to have been buried in 253 separate burial places. On 6 January 2011, the bodies of 12 people were found in a mass grave near an old police station in Mutki, [Bitlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitlis). A few months later, three other mass graves were reportedly found in the garden of Çemişgezek police station.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361]

In 2006, the former ambassador Rouleau stated that the continuing human rights abuses of ethnic Kurds is one of the main obstacles to [Turkish membership of the EU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Turkey_to_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-housegov-362]

#### Illegal abductions and enforced disappearances

During the 1990s and onward Turkish security services have detained Kurds, in some cases they were never seen again with only eyewitnesses coming forward to tell the story.[^cite_note-hrw-disappearances-363] In 1997, Amnesty International (AI) reported that disappearances and extrajudicial executions had emerged as new patterns of human rights violations by the Turkish state.[^cite_note-AmnestyChapter2-364][^cite_note-HRW-disappearances2-365]

The Stockholm Center for Freedom (SCF) documented eleven cases since 2016 in which people have been abducted by men identifying themselves as police officers. It appears to be mostly in the Turkish capital of Ankara as victims are forced into transit vans. Family members were unable to find out their locations from the state, indicating that they were detained secretly or by clandestine groups. In a case where one was finally located after 42 days missing, he was tortured for days, forced to sign a confession and handed over to police.[^cite_note-scf-disappearances-366]

#### Torture

In August 1992, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) reported the practice of torture by security forces in Turkey. The victims of torture interviewed by Helsinki Watch had revealed the systematic practice of torture against detainees in police custody. Sixteen people had died in suspicious circumstances in police custody, ten of them Kurds in the Southeast.[^cite_note-hrwrep1993-357]

In 2013, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* reported that the rape and torture of Kurdish prisoners in Turkey are commonplace. According to the report, published by Amnesty International in 2003, Hamdiye Aslan, a prisoner accused of supporting the Kurdish group, the PKK, had been detained in Mardin Prison, south-east Turkey, for almost three months in which she was reportedly blindfolded, anally raped with a truncheon, threatened and mocked by officers.[^cite_note-gg-367]

In February 2017, a report published by the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) stated the Turkish authorities had beaten and punched detainees, using sexual violence, including rape and threat of rape. In some cases, the detainees were photographed nude and threatened with [public humiliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_humiliation) after being tortured by [Turkish authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_army).[^cite_note-un122017-368]

#### Executions

On 24 February 1992, Cengiz Altun, the Batman correspondent for the weekly pro-Kurdish newspaper, *[Yeni Ülke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeni_%C3%9Clke),* was killed.[^cite_note-369] More than 33 Kurdish journalists working for different newspapers were killed between 1990 and 1995. The killings of Kurdish journalists had started after the pro-Kurdish press had started to publish the first daily newspaper by the name of "[Özgür Gündem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96zg%C3%BCr_G%C3%BCndem)" (Free Agenda). [Musa Anter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musa_Anter), a prominent Kurdish intellectual and journalist of Özgur Gundem, was assassinated by members of [Gendarmerie Intelligence Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie_Intelligence_Organization) in 1992.

In 1992, Turkish security forces executed seventy-four people in house raids and more than a hundred people in demonstrations.[^cite_note-hrwrep1993-357]

In October 2016, amateur footage emerged showing Turkish soldiers executing two female PKK members they had captured alive.[^cite_note-370]

In February 2017, the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) published a report condemning the Turkish government for carrying out systematic executions, displacing civilians, and raping and torturing detainees in [Southeastern Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Turkey).[^cite_note-un122017-368]

In October 2019, nine people were executed, including [Hevrin Khalaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hevrin_Khalaf), a 35-year-old Kurdish woman who was secretary-general of the Future Syria Party and who worked for interfaith unity.[^cite_note-371]

#### Drug trafficking

The Turkish state has similarly involved itself with drug trafficking through the conflict. During the [Susurluk scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susurluk_scandal) in the mid-1990s, it was revealed the government of [Tansu Çiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansu_%C3%87iller) had employed contract killers from the Grey Wolves and [Turkish mafia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_mafia) to assassinate between 2,500 and 5,000 members of and businessmen accused of supporting the PKK.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Later on, state officials used the organizations to collect profits from the heroin trade, an industry larger than the state budget at the time.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] In 2021, the [Turkish-backed Free Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_National_Army) was reported to be planting [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis) in the countryside of [Afrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrin_Region), an area captured from Kurdish forces during [Operation Euphrates Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Euphrates_Shield).[^cite_note-376]

### Abuses by the Kurdish side



A primary school arsoned by PKK militants on 6 January 2016

The [Kurdistan Workers' Party](69680.md) has faced international condemnation for using [terrorist tactics](30636.md), which include [kidnapping](169376.md), civilian [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre), [summary executions](1227309.md), [suicide bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_bomber), and [child soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_soldier), and for its involvement in [drug trafficking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_traffic).[^cite_note-\:11-94][^cite_note-hrwf.eu-95][^cite_note-\:12-377] The organization has targeted civilians, doctors, teachers, schools, hospitals, and other government institutions on a mass scale since the 1984, and is responsible for thousands of civilian deaths.[^cite_note-davidp1-122][^cite_note-\:13-378] The number of total civilian deaths perpetrated by the PKK between 1989 and 1999 was determined as 1,205 by the independent Uppsala One-Sided Violence Dataset.[^cite_note-belge-337][^cite_note-379][^cite_note-(M._Aliza,_2007)-380][^cite_note-satana-381] In 1999, a report published by [HRW](46530.md), stated that the PKK was believed to have been responsible for more than 768 [executions](5902.md) only between 1992 and 1995. The organization had also reportedly committed 25 massacres between 1992 and 1995, killing 360 innocent people, including 39 women and 76 children.[^cite_note-\:15-382] According to Nil Satana, the author of the Kurdish Issue in June 2011 Elections, PKK attacks on civilians persisted until the organization realized that these were damaging their international prestige.[^cite_note-satana-381]

The PKK saw schools as "emblems of Turkish imperialism" that belonged to the "colonial assimilation system" and a justification for the killing of teachers was that they taught Turkish to Kurdish children.[^cite_note-davidp1-122]

&#x20;In the early 1990s, the organization allegedly began to bomb civilian targets and commit massacres against innocent civilians after the government refused to negotiate. According to Jessica Stanton, an associate professor in the global policy area, the shift in PKK tactics was a direct response to government behavior. [Abdullah Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_%C3%96calan), the organization's leader, has been claimed to have stated publicly:&#x20;

> If attacks on military and police targets could not force the government to negotiate, then perhaps attacks on civilian targets would.[^cite_note-Stanton-383]

According to [Amnesty International](18947898.md) reports in 1997, the PKK has tortured and killed Kurdish peasants and its own members that were against them in the 1980s. Dozens of Kurdish civilians have been abducted and killed because they were suspected of being collaborators or informers.[^cite_note-AmnestyChapter3-384] According to a 1996 report by Amnesty International, "in January 1996 the \[Turkish] government announced that the PKK had massacred 11 men near the remote village of [Güçlükonak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BC%C3%A7l%C3%BCkonak). Seven of the victims were members of the local [village guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Village_guard) forces".[^cite_note-amntr-385][^cite_note-dhab-386] The organization's 'suicide guerrilla teams', mainly made up of women, were responsible for 21 [suicide attacks in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_suicide_attacks_in_Turkey) between 1995 and 1999.[^cite_note-davidp1-122] The same number was 11 between 2 August 2015 and 25 August 2016.[^cite_note-\:9-387]

#### Massacres

On 23 January 1987, PKK militants committed a massacre in Ortabağ, [Şırnak Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C4%B1rnak_Province) killing 8 civilians including 4 women and 2 children.[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390]

On 20 June 1987, the organization committed [a massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%B1narc%C4%B1k_massacre) in the village of Pınarcık in the [Mardin Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardin_Province) of [Turkey](11125639.md), killing more than 30 people, mainly women and children.[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392]

On 18 August 1987, PKK militants massacred 14 children, including one three-day-old and one six-day-old baby, and 11 adults in Kılıçkaya village, [Siirt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siirt).[^cite_note-393]

On 10 June 1990, a group of militants attacked on Çevrimli village in Şırnak's Güçlükonak district, killing more than 27 people, most of them women and children. The event is known as Çevrimli massacre.[^cite_note-394]



[February 2016 Ankara bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2016_Ankara_bombing) which killed 30 people and injured 60 others

On 25 December 1991, PKK attacked a store in the Bakırköy district with Molotov cocktails, resulting in 11 deaths, including 7 women and 1 child.[^cite_note-395]

Between 1992 and 1995 the organisation has committed 25 massacres killing 360 innocent people, including 39 women and 76 children.[^cite_note-\:15-382]

On 22 June 1992, a group of PKK militants killed fourteen villagers, nine of them children, and wounded eight others in raids on the houses of village guards in the village of Seki in [Batman Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batman_Province) and in Guroymak in [Bitlis Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitlis_Province).[^cite_note-\:10-396]

On 26 June 1992, 30 PKK militants attacked a mosque in [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r) and killed 10 worshippers.[^cite_note-\:10-396]

In late June 1992, PKK militants killed five people, including a member of the village guard, in an attack on Elmasirti village in [Bingöl Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing%C3%B6l_Province).[^cite_note-\:10-396]

In late September 1992, PKK militants massacred 29 civilians including many women and children in [Cevizdalı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cevizdal%C4%B1,_Bitlis) village of [Bitlis Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitlis_Province).[^cite_note-\:10-396]

On 24 May 1993, a group of 150 PKK militants [massacred 33 unarmed recruits and 5 civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_24,_1993,_PKK_attack) on the [Elazığ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaz%C4%B1%C4%9F)-[Bingöl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing%C3%B6l) highway.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398]

On 21 October 1993, a group of PKK militants killed 22 people, including 13 children in the yard of the school in Siirt. The incident is called Derince massacre.[^cite_note-\:14-399]

On 25 October 1993, 4 teachers and a 2-year-old girl were murdered by PKK militants in Yolalan, [Bitlis Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitlis_Province).[^cite_note-400]

In 1998, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated in a letter to the Italian Prime Minister [Massimo D'Alema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massimo_D'Alema) that in response of Turkeys declaration of an all-out war, the PKK adapted its tactics in the following way:[^cite_note-\:18-401]

- All economic, political, military, security institutions, formations and nationalist organizations—and those who serve in them—have become targets.

  [^cite_note-\:18-401]

  &#x20;PKK has attacked Turkish authorities outside of Kurdish areas.

- The PKK is against Turkish political parties, cultural and educational institutions, legislative and representative bodies, and "all local collaborators and agents working for the Republic of Turkey".

  [^cite_note-\:18-401]

- Many who died were unarmed civilians, caught in the middle between the PKK and security forces, targeted for attacks by both sides.

  [^cite_note-hrwdevelopments-351]

On 21 January 1994, PKK militants massacred 21 people including 11 children in [Savur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savur), [Mardin Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardin_Province) and claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-402]

On 1 January 1995, PKK militants carried out a massacre in the Hamzali neighborhood of Kulp in Diyarbakir, killing 1 village guard and more than 20 civilians, mainly women and children. The memorial has been created for the victims in the neighborhood.[^cite_note-403]

According to a 1996 report by Amnesty International, "in January 1996 the \[Turkish] government announced that the PKK had massacred 11 men near the remote village of [Güçlükonak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BC%C3%A7l%C3%BCkonak). Seven of the victims were members of the local [village guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Village_guard) forces".[^cite_note-amntr-385][^cite_note-dhab-386]

On 30 June 1996, [a suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Tunceli_bombing) targeting a group of 60 unarmed military personnel perpetrated by PKK occurred in [Tunceli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunceli), killing 8 people and injuring 29 others.

On 13 March 1999, 3 PKK militants poured [petroleum](23195.md) and threw [molotov cocktails](20922.md) at a small sized local [department store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_store) in [Göztepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6ztepe,_Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y), [Istanbul](3391396.md), 13 civilians, mostly women and employees of the store, were killed and 5 were injured.[^cite_note-ind-404]

On 22 May 2007, a PKK militant Güven Akkuş carried out [a suicide attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Ankara_bombing) in [Ulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulus,_Ankara), [Altındağ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt%C4%B1nda%C4%9F) killing 9 people and injuring more than 121 others, all civilians.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407]

On 22 June 2010, the TAK claimed responsibility for a bombing on a bus carrying soldiers and civilians killing 5.[^cite_note-408]

On 20 September 2011, 3 people died and 34 people were injured in [a bomb attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Ankara_bombing) in [Ankara](802.md) which was claimed by TAK.[^cite_note-409]

On 18 October 2011, a bomb planted by PKK militants exploded in [Güroymak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BCroymak), killing five policemen and three civilians, including a child.[^cite_note-410]

On 13 March 2016, a member of the TAK carried out [a suicide attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2016_Ankara_bombing) in [Ankara](802.md), killing more than 37 civilians.[^cite_note-411]

On 27 April 2016, Eser Cali, a female militant of the TAK, [blew herself up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Bursa_bombing) near an Ottoman-era mosque in the Turkish city of [Bursa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursa), wounding 13 people. Two days later, the Kurdish militant group TAK claimed the responsibility.[^cite_note-412]

On 10 May 2016, three people were killed and 33 civilians and 12 police officers were injured in [a bombing in Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2016_Diyarbak%C4%B1r_bombing) perpetrated by PKK members who targeted an armored police vehicle.[^cite_note-\:04-413]

On 12 May 2016, [a truck bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2016_D%C3%BCr%C3%BCml%C3%BC_bombing) took place in Dürümlü hamlet in [Diyarbakir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbakir)'s [Sur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sur,_Diyarbak%C4%B1r) district. killing 16 people and injuring 23 others, all civilians. The [People's Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Defence_Forces) (HPG), claimed that the truck was driven by militants of the HPG, but that the explosives should have detonated elsewhere and the truck only detonated as villagers opened fire on the truck.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-\:05-415][^cite_note-416]

On 7 June 2016, [a bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2016_Istanbul_bombing) targeting security officers perperated by TAK occurred in central [Istanbul](3391396.md), killing 12 people including six security officers and six civilians and injuring 51 others,[^cite_note-sabah-417] three of them seriously.[^cite_note-t24-418]

On 5 January 2017, PKK militants detonated a [car bomb](155282.md) outside a courthouse in [Bayraklı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayrakl%C4%B1), [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir) killing a civilian and a police officer while injuring seven others including 3 civilians.[^cite_note-News-419]

On 26 October 2020, [a suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_%C4%B0skenderun_bombing) in İskenderun injured 2 civilians.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423]

#### Kidnappings

Some victims like Esengul Akgul, a child soldier who had been allegedly kidnapped when she was only ten years old in 1990, were described as model 'revolutionary fighters' when they died.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-gender-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427]

In early September 1992, PKK militants kidnapped five tourists, including three [Americans](19792942.md), two [Austrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrians) and a [British](19097669.md), near the town of [Karliova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl%C4%B1ova), [Bingöl Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing%C3%B6l_Province), and detained them briefly.[^cite_note-\:10-396]

A report published by [Federation of American Scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_American_Scientists) stated that the PKK's policy of forced recruitment by kidnapping has dramatically increased since the 1994. The organization has used the policy to offset its heavy losses since the early days of the conflict.[^cite_note-428]

In 2014, [a group of Kurdish families staged a sit-in in front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mothers_of_Diyarbak%C4%B1r) of the town hall in the southeastern Turkish province of [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r) to protest the forced recruitment of their children by PKK. After two weeks of protesting, the families started [hunger strike](222292.md) to demand the return of their kidnapped children.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430]

On 28 May 2012, a group of militants kidnapped 10 workers working on a road construction project in [Iğdır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I%C4%9Fd%C4%B1r). A month later, another group of militants blocked the road between [Diyarbakır](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r) and [Bingöl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bing%C3%B6l), and kidnapped a British tourist. All of them were released later.[^cite_note-431]

**Terrorism**

In 1997, the [State Department](31975.md) listed the PKK as a foreign [terrorist](30636.md) organization for their continuous use of violence during the 1990s.[^cite_note-432] On 6 November 2018, the State Department listed the PKK's three top executives, [Murat Karayılan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Karay%C4%B1lan), [Cemil Bayik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cemil_Bayik) and [Duran Kalkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duran_Kalkan), in its [Rewards for Justice Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rewards_for_Justice_Program) which is developed by the U.S. Department of State for counter-terrorism. The program lists the names and information of the most wanted US designated terrorists in the world.[^cite_note-433]

#### Executions

[Abdullah Öcalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_%C3%96calan), the leader of the organization, who captured power by brutally suppressing dissent and purging opponents after the PKK's third Congress, consolidated absolute power through a campaign of torture and executions he started against the closest cadres in 1980. Only in 1986, the PKK executed more than sixty of its members.[^cite_note-davidp1-122] The organization also targeted the defectors and assassinated at least eight of them in the [EU](9317.md). Hüseyin Yıldırım, a lawyer and the PKK's former spokesman in [Brussels](3708.md), who broke with Öcalan and left the organization in 1987 stated:[^cite_note-434]

> The PKK executed many of its members. The revolutionaries I knew, whom I trusted, were shot. Many people, regardless of whether they were women or children, were killed in the country. Öcalan wanted to be accepted through violence. Many people were killed in [Bekaa Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bekaa_valley) (old training camps). If you dig, you will find corpses.

Many governors and other politicians of AK Party in Kurdish inhabited cities were threatened with death and forced to resign by the PKK, while many politicians who refused to resign were executed and assassinated. Only between August 2016 and October 2016, 6 AK Party politicians were murdered by the organization such as [Deryan Aktert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Deryan_Aktert). In September 2016, AK Party governors of [Doğubayazıt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Do%C4%9Fubayaz%C4%B1t), [Özalp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96zalp,_Van) and [Lice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lice,_Turkey) resigned because of the death threats while 3 other AK Party politicians in [Ergani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ergani) resigned in the first week of October 2016.[^cite_note-435]

In March 1984, 4 people from the same family were executed in [Çukurca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87ukurca). [Serxwebûn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serxweb%C3%BBn) accused them of being informers.[^cite_note-436]

On 15 May 1987, İsa Karaaslan, a father of 3 children and a teacher was accused of being a spy for the Turkish Intelligence and was executed.[^cite_note-\:8-437][^cite_note-438]

On 21 August 1987, in Yolçatı village of [Lice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lice,_Turkey), teacher Asim Özmen[^cite_note-\:8-437] and imam Mehmet Bayram were taken from their houses and executed. Serxwebûn accused both of them of being spies.[^cite_note-439]

On 17 July 1987, 5 people were executed by PKK in Şırnak Province. Serxwebûn, a publication of the PKK announced the executions and stated that executed people were all "traitors".[^cite_note-\:06-440]

On 31 July 1987, Serxwebûn announced that PKK executed Hıdır Kılıçaslan in Kuyubaşı hamlet of Akören village in Hozat and accused him of being an "ungrained traitor".[^cite_note-\:06-440]

Between 1992 and 1995, PKK carried out 768 extrajudicial executions, mostly of civil servants and teachers, political opponents, off-duty police officers and soldiers, and those deemed by the PKK to be "state supporters".[^cite_note-\:15-382]

On 12 February 2006, PKK's former representative of Europe, Kani Yılmaz was assassinated by PKK via a bomb that was put into his car. Yılmaz was burned to death in the car with a former PKK militant Sabri Tori.[^cite_note-Turkishweekly2-441]

On 27 September 2017, the organization kidnapped and executed Mahmut Bazancir who was mistakenly accused of being an informer.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

On 25 November 2017, PKK executed 2 of its own members in [Dohuk Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dohuk_Governorate) of Iraq.[^cite_note-444]

On 25 July 2018, Mevlüt Bengi, a father of 6 children, was executed and tied to an electricity tower by guerrillas, who reportedly justified the execution by accusing him of being a collaborator with the AK Party, which he had served as an election observer at the ballot boxes in his district during the [24 June elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Turkish_parliamentary_election).[^cite_note-445] The HPG released a statement that it has killed Mevlüt Bengi, but for having caused serious harm to the Kurdish movement, and not for being a member of a certain party.[^cite_note-446]

#### Child soldiers

According to the TEPAV think-tank which did research on the identities of 1,362 PKK fighters who lost their lives between 2001 and 2011, 42% of the recruits were under 18, with over a quarter of these being under 15 years of age at the time of recruiting.[^cite_note-447] The organization is still actively recruiting child soldiers and it has been accused of abducting more than 2,000 children by Turkish Security Forces. The latest independent reports by the [Human Rights Watch (HRW)](46530.md), the [United Nations (UN)](31769.md) and the [Amnesty International](18947898.md) have confirmed the recruitment and use of child soldiers by the organization and its armed wings since the 1990s. The organization is also believed to have used the children in the drug trade.[^cite_note-hrwf.eu-95][^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450]

The armed wing of the PKK, signed in October 2008 a Deed of Commitment of the [Geneva Call](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Call), which prohibits the employment of youth below 18 years old to be recruited.[^cite_note-451] Following repeated reports in the international media about child soldiers in PKK ranks, representatives of Geneva Call visited PKK camps in order to monitor the application of the Deed of Commitment.[^cite_note-\:16-452] They visited camps in which youths of the age between sixteen and eighteen would be accepted on a voluntary basis and while being there would stay away from armed conflict and be educated.[^cite_note-\:16-452]

#### Drug trafficking

In 2011, the report published by the [United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_on_Drugs_and_Crime), based on Turkish official reports from The Turkish Addiction Monitoring Centre stated that the instability in Iraq had helped the PKK to develop and use Iraq as a transhipment point for heroin. The PKK was reported by this source to collect taxes per kilogram of heroin trafficked to Turkey from the borders of Islamic Republic of Iran and Iraq, with potential profits reaching US\$200 million annually.[^cite_note-453] Another report published by [European Police Office (EUROPOL)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EUROPOL) in the same year stated the organization is actively involved in money laundering, illicit drugs and human trafficking, as well as illegal immigration inside and outside the EU for funding and running its activities, without citing any official source or specific investigation by justice or police.[^cite_note-europa.eu-454]

In 2012, the U.S. Department of the Treasury's [Office of Foreign Assets Control](231045.md) (OFAC) announced the designation of Zeyneddin Geleri, Cerkez Akbulut (a.k.a. Cernit Murat), and Omer Boztepe as three Moldovan-based specially designated narcotics traffickers for [drug trafficking](218449.md) on behalf of the PKK in [Europe](9239.md). According to the OFAC, Zeyneddin Geleri was identified as a high-ranking member of the PKK while two others were reportedly just PKK activists. The OFAC stated that the [drug trafficking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_traffic) is one of the PKK's criminal activities it uses to obtain weapons and materials to fight the Turkish government.[^cite_note-455]
